Comparison with the nonlocal setting. Formula (4.31) is the analytic engine of the entire diagram: it is what allows Γ2 to be read as “Hessian plus curvature”, and it is the pointwise density of the entropy Hessian on Wasserstein space computed in (4.23) of Sect. 4.1.4. Its availability rests on the diffusion (chain-rule) property of 𝐿 . For the jump generators of Sect. 4.3 the identity (4.31) has no counterpart — Γ2 does not close, and 

𝛼 -stable semigroups satisfy no 𝐶𝐷 (𝐾, ∞) for any 𝐾 — so the top node of Fig. 4.1 is absent, and with it the whole cascade of curvature-based inequalities. On finite graphs (Sect. 4.2) the top node is recovered by a different route: bypassing Bochner, one defines 

the entropic Ricci bound as the convexity constant of the entropy along W-geodesics (Sect. 4.2.3) and thereby restores discrete analogues of LSI , T2, and HWI , with the same implication pattern as in Fig. 4.1. 

Γ-convergence to optimal transport. Let E𝜀 (𝜇 0, 𝜇 1) denote the entropic cost, the optimal value of (4.2) for the Wiener reference of variance 𝜀 (suitably normalised). As 

𝜀 → 0+,

𝜀 E𝜀 (𝜇 0, 𝜇 1) Γ

−−→ 12 𝑊 22 (𝜇 0, 𝜇 1), (4.33) minimisers converge to optimal transport plans, and the entropic interpolation 𝜌 𝜀 𝑡 con-verges to McCann’s displacement interpolation — the 𝑊 2 geodesic [41, 143]. The Schrödinger problem is thus a canonical regularisation of optimal transport, and con-versely optimal transport is the zero-temperature shadow of the bridge; the stability of this picture under perturbations of the reference measure [91] will be essential when the reference becomes a Lévy process in Sect. 4.3. The nonlocal analogue of (4.33) — the localisation limit — is Theorem 4.6 below, and its numerical face is Fig. 4.3. 

4.1.6 Extension: Changing the Base Geometry 

The constructions of this section used remarkably little about the Wiener reference: an entropy, a Markov semigroup, and a Girsanov-type energy identity. The variational principle (4.2) is therefore stable under changes of the base geometry , and the standard 112 4 Schrödinger Bridges and Information Geodesics in the Space of Probability Densities 

extensions of the theory are best understood as three instances of this one principle: change the underlying space, change the time axis, or change the level of description of the particles. 

Changing the space: Riemannian and sub-Riemannian bridges. Replacing R𝑑 

by a Riemannian manifold replaces the Wiener measure by the law of manifold Brow-nian motion; the entire apparatus — Schrödinger system, ( 𝑓 , 𝑔 )-transform, FP–HJB coupling — carries over with the Laplace–Beltrami operator, and the resulting diffu-sion Schrödinger bridges generate data constrained to spheres, matrix manifolds, and beyond [208]. More is true: on a sub-Riemannian manifold, where admissible veloci-ties are restricted to a bracket-generating horizontal distribution, hypoellipticity guar-antees smooth strictly positive transition densities, the forward–backward characteri-sation of the bridge persists, and the vanishing-noise limit recovers deterministic sub-Riemannian optimal transport [208]. The lesson is structural: the bridge problem needs only a well-behaved heat kernel, not ellipticity. 

Changing the time axis: the quantum correspondence. The forward–backward pair underlying (4.16)–(4.17) can be written, for a bridge in a potential 𝑉 , as 

𝜕 𝑡 𝜑 = − 12 Δ𝜑 + 𝑉 𝜑, 𝜕 𝑡 ˆ𝜑 = 12 Δ ˆ𝜑 − 𝑉 ˆ𝜑, 𝜌 = 𝜑 ˆ𝜑. (4.34) Under the Wick rotation 𝑡 7 → 𝑖𝑡 these become the forward and backward Schrödinger equations of quantum mechanics, the factors 𝜑, ˆ𝜑 become the conjugate wavefunctions 

𝜓, 𝜓 ∗, and the Born rule 𝜌 = |𝜓 |2 becomes the product formula of Theorem 4.1. The cor-respondence runs deep: the control-affine bridge problem transforms into a two-point boundary value problem for a Schrödinger PDE with a complex potential whose real part generalises the Bohm potential — the same object that drives Newton’s equation (4.24) — and whose imaginary part encodes absorption [207]. Bridges with genuinely quantum (unitary) dynamics admit closed-form Gaussian solutions [26]; see also Prob-lem 4.3. 

Changing the particle level: mean-field bridges. Replacing independent particles by a weakly interacting population replaces the reference by a McKean–Vlasov diffusion 

d𝑋 𝑡 = (𝑏 (𝑋 𝑡 ) +∇ 𝑈 ∗𝜌 𝑡 (𝑋 𝑡 )) d𝑡 +√2𝜀 d𝑊 𝑡 , and the mean-field Schrödinger bridge seeks the minimum-effort steering of the population between prescribed marginals [72]. The optimality system is (4.18) augmented by nonlocal interaction terms, 

𝜕 𝑡 𝜌 + ∇· (𝜌 (∇ 𝜑 + ∇ 𝑈 ∗ 𝜌 )) = 𝜀 Δ𝜌, 𝜕 𝑡 𝜑 + 12 |∇ 𝜑 |2 + ∇ 𝑈 ∗ 𝜌 · ∇ 𝜑 = −𝜀 Δ𝜑, (4.35) a nonconvex problem in general; a generalized Hopf–Cole transform linearises the in-teraction under symmetry and Lipschitz assumptions on 𝑈 , and Sinkhorn-type recur-sions then apply [72]. The same coupled system appears in variational mean-field games [31], with the boundary conditions — two-point in time for bridges, initial-terminal for games — marking the only structural difference. Conceptually, the mean-field exten-sion changes the base space from X to (a fibre over) P (X) : it is again the same entropy principle, one level higher still. 4.2 The Nonlocal Theory I: Probability Simplex 113 

# 4.2 The Nonlocal Theory I: Probability Simplex 

We now leave the local world. The nonlocal setting in which the second-order theory is complete, is that of a Markov jump process on a finite graph. Recall from Remark 4.1 that the forward equation here, the master equation, is spatially first order: it involves only single differences 𝜌 𝑦 − 𝜌 𝑥 . This section shows that spatial order is no obstacle to temporal order: the discrete setting supports the full arc of Sect. 4.1, from the stochastic viewpoint through functional inequalities to explicit curvature, Jacobi equations, and Hamiltonian flows. Everything is finite-dimensional, and everything can be computed. Throughout, X is a finite set, 𝑞 𝑥 𝑦 ≥ 0 are jump rates of an irreducible continuous-time Markov chain, reversible with respect to a probability measure 𝜋 (detailed balance: 

𝜋 𝑥 𝑞 𝑥 𝑦 = 𝜋 𝑦 𝑞 𝑦 𝑥 ). Densities are vectors 𝜌 ∈ P (X) , identified with points of the simplex. 

4.2.1 The Stochastic-Analysis Viewpoint 

The stochastic side of the theory transfers verbatim from Sect. 4.1.1: with 𝑅 the law of the reversible chain on path space 𝐷 ([ 0, 1]; X) , the dynamic Schrödinger problem (4.2) is well posed, the Schrödinger system (4.9)–(4.10) becomes a pair of equations for vectors 𝑓 0, 𝑔 1 ∈ RX≥0 coupled through the transition matrix 𝑅 01 = 𝑒 𝑄 (with 𝑄 the rate matrix), and the optimal bridge is the ( 𝑓 , 𝑔 )-transform 𝑃 ★ = 𝑓 0 (𝑋 0)𝑔 1 (𝑋 1) 𝑅 , an 

ℎ-transformed, time-inhomogeneous jump chain with the same jump structure as the reference. Fortet’s contraction argument applies directly, and convergence of the alter-nating scheme of Remark 4.2 is geometric in Hilbert’s projective metric [90]; in matrix language the scheme is diagonal matrix scaling, and we shall say no more about com-putation. 

The logarithmic-mean chain rule. The ODE-level structure is more interesting, because it is here that the nonlocal world first genuinely differs from the local one. The local theory rests on the chain rule ∇𝜌 = 𝜌 ∇ log 𝜌 , which dictates the mobility 𝜌 in the continuity equation. On a graph there is no chain rule: a difference of values is not a value times a difference of logarithms. The resolution is an interpolation. For 𝑎, 𝑏 > 0

let

𝜌 𝑦 − 𝜌 𝑥 = Λ log (𝜌 𝑥 , 𝜌 𝑦 ) (log 𝜌 𝑦 − log 𝜌 𝑥 

). (4.37) Identity (4.37) is the discrete chain rule, and the logarithmic mean is the price of non-locality.

The rigidity claim will matter below: for metric-topological questions other interpola-tions (arithmetic, geometric) are perfectly admissible and are used in Sect. 4.3, but the gradient-flow identification pins the interpolation down uniquely. This is the discrete precursor of the dichotomy running through the whole nonlocal theory.

Formula (4.39) is a verbatim discrete Benamou–Brenier formula: kinetic action, mobility-weighted, minimised under a continuity constraint. Its analytical content, how-ever, is finite-dimensional: on the interior of the simplex, W is the geodesic distance of a genuine Riemannian metric with smooth, explicit metric tensor. Geodesics solve finite-dimensional Hamiltonian ODEs; existence and (interior) smoothness are elemen-tary. The metric degenerates at the boundary of the simplex, but — in sharp contrast with the continuous nonlocal setting of Theorem ?? below — the boundary remains at 

finite distance: Dirac masses can be connected, as Example 4.1 computes explicitly. The construction extends beyond linear equations. Replacing the logarithmic mean in (4.39) by the mean 𝜍 𝜑 (𝑎, 𝑏 ) adapted to a convex function 𝜑 , Erbar and Maas [76] exhibited the discrete porous medium equation

as the gradient flow of the corresponding internal energy: the entire gradient-flow zoo of Sect. 4.1.3 has a discrete counterpart, mean by mean. Finally, the discrete and local geometries are not merely analogous but connected: on the lattice 1

4.2.3 The Geometric Viewpoint II: Second-Order 

Because ( ˚P (X) , W) is a finite-dimensional Riemannian manifold with an explicit met-ric tensor, the second-order theory is a matter of calculus rather than of definition. Three layers deserve record. 

Curvature. Following Lott–Sturm–Villani in reverse, Erbar and Maas [75] define 

the entropic Ricci curvature of the chain as the best constant 𝜅 such that the entropy is 

𝜅 -convex along W-geodesics, 

Hess W 𝐻 (𝜌 | 𝜋 ) ≥ 𝜅 W-metric , (4.40) and then, crucially, compute it: the Hessian on the left is an explicit finite-dimensional quadratic form (no Bochner formula is needed — this is the decisive advantage of finite dimension), and lower bounds follow for concrete chains. For the two-point space with unit rates, 𝜅 = 2 exactly (verified numerically in Example 4.1); for the discrete hyper-cube {0, 1}𝑛 , 𝜅 = 2/𝑛 ; discrete tori and birth–death chains are likewise explicit [75]. Through Sect. 4.2.4, each such bound yields MLSI, Talagrand and HWI inequalities with explicit constants. 

Connection, parallel transport, Jacobi fields. On the interior of the simplex the Christoffel symbols of the metric (4.39) are explicit rational expressions in 𝜌 (ratio-nal in 𝜌 and in the values Λlog (𝜌 𝑥 , 𝜌 𝑦 ) and their partial derivatives), so parallel trans-port, geodesic deviation, Jacobi fields and conjugate points are all governed by finite-dimensional linear ODEs along geodesics. Nothing here is deep — and that is precisely the point to be contrasted with Sect. 4.3.4, where none of these objects is even defined. 

Hamiltonian flows and Newton’s equation. The discrete analogue of the second-order dynamics of Sect. 4.1.4 is furnished by Wasserstein Hamiltonian flows on graphs [48]: for a Hamiltonian H ( 𝜌, 𝜑 ) = 12

# 4.3 The Nonlocal Theory II: Continuous State Spaces under Lévy Noise 

The continuous nonlocal theory replaces the Brownian reference of Sect. 4.1 by a Lévy process — a pure-jump process or a jump-diffusion — and the finite graph of Sect. 4.2 by R𝑑 . The forward equations are now partial integro-differential equations of (fractional) second-order type, such as the fractional heat equation (Remark 4.1). The section follows the arc of its two predecessors: bridges exist and are well understood at the stochastic level (Sect. 4.3.1); the Partial Integral-Differential Equation (PIDE) characterisation extends, including killing and unbalanced marginals (Sect. 4.3.2); the first-order geometry — nonlocal Wasserstein distances, their surprising topological tri-chotomy, and the localisation limit back to 𝑊 2 — is by now a mature theory (Sect. 4.3.3), completed by first functional inequalities (Sect. 4.3.5). The second-order theory, how-ever, breaks: Sect. 4.3.4 records what exists, what fails, and why, in the form of open problems. The worked example of Sect. 4.3.6 makes the difference between local and nonlocal geodesics visible in a single picture. 118 4 Schrödinger Bridges and Information Geodesics in the Space of Probability Densities

Second, Privault and Zambrini further show that the processes so constructed are, under suitable conditions on the Bernstein kernel, exactly the reciprocal Markov processes with jumps, and they characterise them variationally through a stochastic control problem whose value function solves a Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation — the jump analogue of the second-order struc-ture of Sect. 4.1.4, not a relative-entropy minimisation. (iii) Stable : bridges of symmetric 𝛼 -stable processes inherit self-similarity, with transition densities obtained by conditioning the stable kernel on the endpoints. These three examples will reappear as the reference processes of the worked example in Sect. 4.3.6.

Bridges for jump-diffusions. Zlotchevski and Chen [231] study the Schrödinger bridge problem — the minimisation of the relative entropy KL (P ‖ R) over path mea-sures with prescribed endpoint marginals — when the reference R is the law of a jump diffusion. Writing (𝔣 , 𝔤 ) for the solution of the associated Schrödinger system and 𝔥 (𝑡, 𝑥 ) = ∫ 𝔤 (𝑦 ) 𝑃 𝑡 ,𝑇 (𝑥, d𝑦 ), their central structural result is that the bridge is an 

ℎ-transform of a reference path measure, 

ˆP = 𝔥 (𝑇, 𝑋 𝑇 )

𝔥 (0, 𝑋 0) P, (4.49) and that, when 𝔥 is harmonic, ˆP solves the martingale problem for a jump-diffusion generator 𝐿 𝔥 obtained from 𝐿 by a shift of the drift and a rescaling of the Lévy measure by 𝔥 (𝑡, 𝑥 + 𝛾 )/ 𝔥 (𝑡, 𝑥 ). Thus the bridge remains a jump diffusion of the same type as the reference; the ℎ-transform structure of the Brownian theory (Theorem 4.1) survives intact in the nonlocal world. When the reference admits a sufficiently regular transition density, the pair (𝜑, ˆ𝜑 ) representing the bridge solves a forward–backward system of PIDEs generalising the local Schrödinger system, and the marginal density of ˆP is the product 𝜑 ˆ𝜑 [231]. What one loses relative to the Brownian case is only the Hopf–Cole 

gain : the transform still linearises the problem, but the resulting equations are nonlocal. A companion paper [232] extends the analysis to jump diffusions with regime switch-ing. There are also some variants and extensions. 

Killing and unbalanced marginals. When the marginals carry unequal mass, no conservative process can connect them. Chen, Georgiou and Pavon [42] resolved this by allowing a killing rate 𝑉 (𝑡, 𝑋 𝑡 ) — a jump to an absorbing graveyard state — and showed that the most likely evolution of diffusing-and-vanishing particles is again a Schrödinger bridge after embedding into a conservative system on the augmented state space. Since killing is a jump, unbalanced transport is naturally a chapter of the nonlocal theory, with applications from single-cell biology to population dynamics. 

Space-fractional transport. A parallel line of work makes the spatial operator non-local. Bai, Guo, Li, Zheng and Zhu [17] introduce a nonlocal dispersive optimal trans-port (NDOT) intended to describe anomalous transport in heterogeneous media, where agents exhibit both long-range spatial jumps and long-time memory. The classical con-tinuity constraint of the Benamou–Brenier problem is replaced by a space–time frac-tional partial differential equation combining a Caputo derivative in time with a frac-tional Laplacian in space, and the transport problem minimises a fractional kinetic en-4.3 The Nonlocal Theory II: Continuous State Spaces under Lévy Noise 121 

ergy under this constraint. The authors solve the NDOT formulation numerically with a general-proximal primal–dual hybrid gradient (G-Prox PDHG) algorithm, together with a preconditioner derived from the discretisation of the space–time fractional PDE to accelerate convergence; their experiments report reduced kinetic-energy cost rela-tive to integer-order transport in heterogeneous media. As with the Schrödinger bridge, we record the model for its structural place in the nonlocal programme and leave the computation aside. 

Quantum bridges. A different nonlocal ingredient enters when the interpolating dynamics is governed by the Schrödinger equation rather than a stochastic differen-tial equation. Bordyuh, Clevert and Bertolini [26] formulate a quantum Schrödinger bridge problem (QSBP) from a Lagrangian, dynamical-optimal-transport perspective, and show that its evolution equations are driven by the Bohm (quantum) potential, which supplies the nonlocality that distinguishes the quantum bridge from classical stochas-tic dynamics. Solving the Fokker–Planck and Hamilton–Jacobi equations that arise in this Lagrangian formulation, they derive exact closed-form solutions between Gaussian endpoints: as in the classical bridge the solution remains a Gaussian process, but the covariance evolves differently on account of the quantum potential. The construction is aimed at generative modelling, and, in keeping with the rest of this chapter, we note it without pursuing the algorithmic side. 

Time-fractional bridges. A distinct nonlocal variant replaces the temporal deriva-tive rather than the spatial one. Li, Li and Wang [145] formulate a multiscale nonlocal-in-time Schrödinger bridge problem , in which the interpolating flow is constrained not by the ordinary continuity equation but by a time-fractional Wasserstein gradient flow with advective flux, the Caputo derivative 𝜕 𝛼 𝑡 (𝛼 ∈ ( 0, 1)) encoding memory and sub-diffusive transport. Writing the first-order optimality conditions for the resulting con-trol problem yields a strongly coupled nonlinear system consisting of a forward time-fractional Fokker–Planck equation and a backward time-fractional Hamilton–Jacobi– Bellman equation — the time-fractional analogue of the forward–backward pair that governs the classical bridge. Relative to that classical analogue, the cost functional car-ries an additional term involving the fractional derivative, which is the source of the genuinely multiscale-in-time behaviour; after a change of variables the problem can be recast as a minimisation constrained by a time-fractional transport equation. The authors solve the system numerically with a G-Prox primal–dual hybrid gradient scheme whose preconditioner is built from the fractional time discretisation. As with the spatial vari-ants above, the construction sits within the nonlocal programme of this chapter — here the nonlocality is in time rather than in space — and we again leave the computational details aside. 

4.3.3 The Geometric Viewpoint I: First-Order 

The geometric viewpoint asks for the analogue, under a jump reference, of Otto’s construction. The answer — a nonlocal Benamou–Brenier formula — was developed by Erbar [74] for the gradient-flow structure and by Slepčev and Warren [201] in the 122 4 Schrödinger Bridges and Information Geodesics in the Space of Probability Densities 

general metric setting; we follow the latter. 

Nonlocal vector calculus. Fix a symmetric jump kernel 𝐽 (𝑥, 𝑦 ) = 𝐽 (𝑦, 𝑥 ) ≥ 0 and an interpolation function Θ : [0, ∞) 2 → [ 0, ∞) , symmetric, jointly concave, posi-tively 1-homogeneous. The nonlocal gradient of a function is ∇𝜑 (𝑥, 𝑦 ) = 𝜑 (𝑦 ) − 𝜑 (𝑥 );the nonlocal divergence of an antisymmetric flux 𝑉 (𝑥, 𝑦 ) = −𝑉 (𝑦, 𝑥 ) is div 𝑉 (𝑥 ) =∫ 𝑉 (𝑥, 𝑦 )𝐽 (𝑥, 𝑦 ) d𝑦 , and a nonlocal integration by parts holds. A curve of measures satisfies the nonlocal continuity equation

(4.51) For the logarithmic mean Θ = Λ log , the jump semigroup with kernel 𝐽 , which is the fractional heat semigroup for 𝐽 (𝑥, 𝑦 ) = |𝑥 − 𝑦 |−𝑑 −2𝑠 , is the gradient flow of the entropy in this metric [74], extending Theorem 4.2 from graphs to R𝑑 ; for translation-invariant kernels the induced distance is comparable to a fractional Sobolev norm. The framework simultaneously contains the discrete metric of Definition 4.3 (take a graph as base space) and, as we shall see, the classical 𝑊 2 as a limit. Notice, however, the discrete lesson at work: only Θ = Λ log yields the gradient-flow identification, while the metric theory below is developed for general Θ.

The topology of W𝐽 , Θ. The topology induced by W𝐽 , Θ depends delicately on the weight kernel near the origin and on the boundary value Θ(1, 0) of the interpolation, through the cost of expelling mass from a Dirac. The key estimate is the following (in the notation of Definition 4.5, 𝐽 is the weight kernel and Θ the interpolation, both assumed isotropic and to satisfy the standing structural assumptions).

Read together, these results show that the geometry of W𝐽 , Θ is governed by two independent switches — whether the kernel 𝐽 is integrable at the origin, and whether the interpolation Θ vanishes on the boundary. When 𝐽 is integrable and Θ(1, 0) = 0,as for the logarithmic mean, the space is highly disconnected: expelling a Dirac costs infinite action, so a delta mass cannot be moved at finite cost. When 𝐽 is integrable but Θ(1, 0) > 0, as for the arithmetic mean, the distance is bi-Lipschitz to √TV 

and metrizes the strong topology. When 𝐽 has a non-integrable algebraic singular-ity 𝐽 (| 𝑦 |) ≳ |𝑦 |−𝑑 −𝑠 , as for the 𝑠 -stable jump kernel driving fractional diffusion, it metrizes the weak topology, exactly as the classical Wasserstein distance does. The disconnectedness of the first regime has no analogue on a finite graph (Sect. 4.2.2): it is a purely infinite-dimensional phenomenon, and the first sign that second-order analysis will be delicate.

Neighbouring geometries. Four constructions extend the same variational template in different directions, and we record them for orientation. Covariance-modulated trans-port [29] weights the kinetic action by the inverse covariance of the current measure,

∫ (𝑣 −¯𝑣 )>Σ−1 

> 𝜌

(𝑣 −¯𝑣 ) d𝜌 , yielding the gradient-flow geometry of mean-field ensem-ble Kalman methods, Gaussianity-preserving and weakly equivalent to 𝑊 2. Configura-tion spaces : on the space of point configurations, Dello Schiavo, Herry and Suzuki [189] built a nonlocal continuity equation from the difference operator of the Poisson space and proved a Benamou–Brenier formula together with Ricci bounds for Poisson mea-sures — a rare instance where curvature statements survive in an infinite-dimensional nonlocal setting, thanks to the exact structure of the Poisson measure. Coarse extrin-sic curvature : Li [156] used 𝑊 1-costs of small balls to define a coarse mean curvature of submanifolds converging to the classical one, bridging optimal transport and extrin-sic geometry. Graph limits : for kernels supported on graphs the framework recovers discrete transport distances, closing the triangle with Sect. 4.2. 

4.3.4 The Geometric Viewpoint II: What Exists, What Breaks, and Open Problems 

On the local side, the second-order theory rests on the Bochner formula; on graphs, on finite dimensionality. The continuous nonlocal setting has neither, and each of the four pillars of Sects. 4.1.4 and 4.2.3 fails for its own reason. 4.3 The Nonlocal Theory II: Continuous State Spaces under Lévy Noise 125 

No Bochner formula. For a jump generator 𝐿 𝑓 (𝑥 ) = ∫ ( 𝑓 (𝑦 ) − 𝑓 (𝑥 )) 𝐽 (𝑥, 𝑦 ) d𝑦 the carré du champ is Γ( 𝑓 )( 𝑥 ) = 12

∫ ( 𝑓 (𝑦 )− 𝑓 (𝑥 )) 2 𝐽 (𝑥, 𝑦 ) d𝑦 , but the iterated form Γ2 does not close: no identity of the type (4.23) expresses it through a Hessian plus a curvature term. Worse, 𝛼 -stable semigroups satisfy no Bakry–Émery condition 𝐶𝐷 (𝐾, ∞) for any 

𝐾 ∈ R: the analytic engine of the local second-order theory is not merely unavailable but provably absent. 

No tangent-space regularity. Second-order calculus in Gigli’s sense [92] presup-poses a robust first-order identification of tangent vectors. Locally, the elliptic equation 

−∇· ( 𝜌 ∇𝜑 ) = §𝜌 delivers it; on graphs, linear algebra does. In the nonlocal continuum, recovering 𝜑 from §𝜌 means inverting the degenerate, 𝜌 -dependent nonlocal operator 

𝜑 7 → div (Θ ∇𝜑 ), for which no regularity theory exists; the tangent space admits only a weak description, and connections, parallel transport and Jacobi fields are not defined .

Topological pathologies. Entire regimes of kernels produce totally disconnected or TV-type geometries in which geodesic calculus is meaningless; even in the weak-topology regime, geodesics are known only through the subsolution duality (4.52), with-out an eikonal equation to differentiate. The deepest obstruction may be structural. Large-deviation analysis of jump pro-cesses produces cosh-type generalized gradient structures [180]: the dissipation poten-tial natural to jumps is not quadratic, so the geometry most compatible with path-space relative entropy — that is, with the Schrödinger problem itself — is of Finsler rather than Riemannian type. If so, the quadratic nonlocal Wasserstein metric of Definition 4.5 is a convenient proxy, and a Levi-Civita connection is not merely hard to build but the wrong object to ask for. These observations sharpen into a list. (Compare each item with its solved discrete counterpart in Sect. 4.2.3.) 

Open Problem 4.1 . Construct a second-order calculus (connection, parallel transport, Jacobi equation) on (P ( R𝑑 ), W𝐽 , Λlog ) for a nontrivial class of kernels, or prove an ob-struction. 

Open Problem 4.2 . Identify a substitute for the Γ2-criterion adapted to jump genera-tors — e.g. convexity of the entropy along W𝐽 , Λlog -geodesics, or a cosh-type curvature notion — strong enough to yield displacement convexity and sharpened functional in-equalities in Sect. 4.3.5. 

Open Problem 4.3 . Derive a Newton equation for Lévy-driven Schrödinger bridges: the analogue of (4.24) with the Fisher information replaced by a nonlocal information functional. 

Open Problem 4.4 . Prove (or disprove) that the small-noise limit of the Lévy Schrödinger bridge is the W𝐽 , Θ-geodesic. The local proof of (4.33) uses Γ-convergence machinery whose nonlocal counterpart, given the cosh-structure of [180], cannot be assumed. This list redeems, in the honest currency of open problems, the promise of curvature, Jacobi fields and conjugate points made at the opening of this chapter. 126 4 Schrödinger Bridges and Information Geodesics in the Space of Probability Densities 

4.3.5 Functional Inequalities in Nonlocal Wasserstein Space 

The pattern of Sects. 4.1.5 and 4.2.4 continues, with partial results. Erbar’s identification of jump semigroups as entropy gradient flows [74] yields energy-dissipation identities — the first-order half of the LSI story — and, for stationary point processes, Huesmann and Stange [114] proved that the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck semigroup is the gradient flow of the specific relative entropy for a nonlocal transport metric and derived HWI- and Talagrand-type inequalities in that setting, together with the existence of solutions of the nonlocal continuity equation between arbitrary stationary point processes. What is conspicuously missing is the third vertex of the local triangle: inequalities whose proof 

requires convexity of the entropy along geodesics — displacement convexity, HWI in its curvature form, sharpened Talagrand constants — because those proofs consume precisely the second-order input that, as the next subsection explains, the continuous nonlocal setting does not possess.

> Bimodality of the midpoint marginal is the observable fingerprint of nonlocality.

Dynamically, this is the transport-geometric shadow of a fact established for sample paths in Chapter 2: exit rates of 𝛼 -stable-driven double-well systems are polynomial in the noise amplitude [117] rather than Kramers-exponential, because Lévy paths do not climb barriers — they jump over them. For applications, the criterion supplies the mechanism behind the early-warning discussion of Problem 4.5: under Lévy perturba-tions the warning signal is not rising variance but the premature condensation of mass at the target basin [223, 229]. Finally, shrinking the kernel range continuously deforms the teleport geodesic into the travelling one — this is Fig. 4.3, already displayed beside Theorem 4.6. Code: Appendices A.2–A.3. 

# 4.4 Information-Geometry 

The transport geometries of Sects. 4.1–4.3 measure the cost of moving mass. There is a second, older geometry on spaces of probability measures that instead measures the cost of telling distributions apart : information geometry , founded by Rao, Chentsov and Amari [5, 6]. The Schrödinger problem sits precisely at the junction of the two, because its objective is a relative entropy — the canonical divergence of information geometry — while its constraint set is transport-theoretic. This section develops the information-geometric language from first principles, assuming no prior exposure, and then shows how the Schrödinger bridge, the Sinkhorn algorithm, and the second-order “acceleration” picture of Sect. 4.1.4 all reappear as information-geometric objects. 128 4 Schrödinger Bridges and Information Geodesics in the Space of Probability Densities 

Fig. 4.5: Example 4.2, the quantitative fingerprint. Left: midpoint marginals 𝜌 1/2; the lo-cal solution is unimodal at 𝑥 = 0 (dashed: analytic displacement interpolation; the mild widening of the numerical curve is grid diffusion), while the heavy-tailed marginal is bimodal with peaks at the endpoint locations ±1. Right: mass in the central “barrier” region along the geodesic: the local curve necessarily exhibits a crossing peak; the non-local curves are flattened. 

4.4.1 Foundations: Fisher Metric, Dual Connections and Divergences 

We begin with the definitions, in the concrete setting of a finite-dimensional parametric model; no differential geometry is presupposed beyond the notion of a smooth surface carrying a metric. 

Statistical manifolds and the Fisher metric. A statistical model is a family 𝑆 =

{𝑝 (· ; 𝜃 ) : 𝜃 ∈ Ξ ⊂ R𝑛 } of probability densities smoothly parametrised by 𝜃 . Treating 

𝜃 as a coordinate system makes 𝑆 an 𝑛 -dimensional manifold, each point of which is 

a probability distribution. Write ℓ𝜃 (𝑥 ) = log 𝑝 (𝑥 ; 𝜃 ) for the log-likelihood and 𝜕 𝑖 =

𝜕 /𝜕𝜃 𝑖 . The score 𝜕 𝑖 ℓ𝜃 has mean zero, E𝜃 [𝜕 𝑖 ℓ𝜃 ] = 0, because ∫ 𝑝 d𝑥 = 1; its covariance is the central object of the theory. 

Definition 4.6 (Fisher information metric) The Fisher metric on 𝑆 is the Riemannian metric with components

The geodesic distance it induces is the Fisher–Rao distance ,the intrinsic Riemannian distance between distributions in the family. 4.4 Information Geometry 129 

A metric alone does not fix a notion of “straight line”; that requires an affine con-nection . Information geometry carries a one-parameter family of them, tied together by a single symmetric 3-tensor.

Dual flatness and Legendre duality. The structure becomes computationally deci-sive when both dual connections are flat at once. 

Definition 4.8 (Dually flat manifold) (𝑆, 𝑔, ∇(1) , ∇(− 1) ) is dually flat if there is a chart 

𝜃 in which ∇(1) has vanishing Christoffel symbols (an 𝑒 -affine chart). Duality then forces a chart 𝜂 in which ∇(− 1) is flat (an 𝑚 -affine chart), and the two are Legendre conjugate: there is a convex potential 𝜓 (𝜃 ) with 

𝜂 𝑖 = 𝜕 𝑖 𝜓 (𝜃 ), 𝜃 𝑖 = 𝜕 𝑖 𝜑 (𝜂 ), 𝜓 (𝜃 ) + 𝜑 (𝜂 ) = Õ𝑖 𝜃 𝑖 𝜂 𝑖 , (4.58) and 𝑔 𝑖 𝑗 = 𝜕 𝑖 𝜕 𝑗 𝜓 in the 𝜃 -chart. The prototype is an exponential family 𝑝 (𝑥 ; 𝜃 ) = exp (Õ𝑖 𝜃 𝑖 𝑇 𝑖 (𝑥 ) − 𝜓 (𝜃 )) : the nat-ural parameter 𝜃 is 𝑒 -affine, the expectation parameter 𝜂 = E𝜃 [𝑇 ] is 𝑚 -affine, the log-partition function 𝜓 is the convex potential, and 𝑔 is its Hessian. In such a chart the 

𝑒 -geodesic between two distributions is a straight line in 𝜃 (a log-linear, geometric-mean path), the 𝑚 -geodesic a straight line in 𝜂 (a mixture, arithmetic-mean path); both are closed-form — the first structural advantage of finite dimensionality.

0 (zero iff 𝑝 = 𝑞 , generally asymmetric and not a metric) measures directed dissimi-larity. On a dually flat manifold the canonical divergence is the Bregman divergence of the potential,

The pay-off is a single theorem. 

Theorem 4.7 (Generalized Pythagorean theorem; 5) Let (𝑆, 𝑔, ∇(1) , ∇(− 1) ) be dually flat with canonical divergence (4.59) . If the 𝑚 -geodesic from 𝑝 to 𝑟 meets the 𝑒 -geodesic from 𝑟 to 𝑞 orthogonally at 𝑟 , then 

𝐷 ( 𝑝 ‖ 𝑞 ) = 𝐷 ( 𝑝 ‖ 𝑟 ) + 𝐷 (𝑟 ‖ 𝑞 ). (4.61) 

Hence the divergence projection of a point onto an 𝑒 -flat (resp. 𝑚 -flat) submanifold is unique and characterised by orthogonality. 

Projections onto flat submanifolds are therefore well-posed and additive — the ge-ometric engine behind maximum likelihood, the EM algorithm, and, as we now show, the Schrödinger bridge. 

4.4.2 The Schrödinger Bridge as an Entropic Projection 

The static bridge is an 𝑒 -projection. The static Schrödinger problem minimises 

𝐷 KL (𝜋 ‖ 𝑅 01 ) over couplings 𝜋 ∈ Π(𝜇 0, 𝜇 1) with prescribed marginals, 𝑅 01 being the reference two-time joint law; this is the entropic optimal-transport problem (4.15). In the language just built, each marginal constraint {𝜋 : 𝜋 0 = 𝜇 0 } and {𝜋 : 𝜋 1 = 𝜇 1 }

is 𝑚 -flat (linear in 𝜋 ), and the minimiser of 𝐷 KL (· ‖ 𝑅 01 ) over their intersection is, by Theorem 4.7, the 𝑒 -projection of 𝑅 01 onto it. That this projection lies on the 𝑒 -flat family { 𝑓 (𝑥 )𝑔 (𝑦 )𝑅 01 (𝑥, 𝑦 )} is exactly the Schrödinger factorisation (4.12): the bridge’s product structure and its characterisation as an 𝑒 -projection are the same statement. 

Sinkhorn as alternating projection. The iterative proportional fitting / Sinkhorn recursion (Remark 4.2) alternately enforces the two marginals; each half-step is a divergence projection onto one 𝑚 -flat constraint set, so the algorithm is an alternating projection between them. Modin [168] gives the geometric reading of this algorithm, emphasising that Sinkhorn is naturally viewed as a discretisation, by standard fixed-point iteration, of a nonlinear integral equation on measures, and connecting it in an appendix to Beurling’s theorem on product measures — the same result underlying the existence of the potentials 𝑓 , 𝑔 . The classical Hilbert-metric contraction proof of 4.4 Information Geometry 131 

convergence thereby acquires a differential-geometric reading. 

An 𝜀 -family interpolating Fisher–Rao and Wasserstein. The entropic cost carries a temperature 𝜀 (the diffusivity of the reference). As 𝜀 → 0 the entropy is negligible beside the transport cost, the problem contracts to Monge transport, and the geometry is the Wasserstein geometry of Sect. 4.1.3 — the Γ-limit (4.33). As 𝜀 → ∞ the entropy dominates, the optimal coupling decorrelates toward the independent product, and a second-order expansion of the entropic cost at coinciding marginals returns the Fisher metric (4.54). The Schrödinger problem thus interpolates between Fisher–Rao (distin-guishability) and Wasserstein (transport) geometry, with 𝜀 the interpolation parameter — the static counterpart of the dynamic zero-noise limit of Sect. 4.1.5. The projection picture is not only conceptual; it organizes a wave of recent algorithms that solve the bridge by restricting it to a finite-dimensional statistical submanifold and inheriting the closed-form calculus. We record the principal instances; computation is left to the cited works. 

Gaussian and Gaussian-mixture bridges. When both marginals are Gaussian, the Schrödinger bridge is available in closed form, its drift and covariance given by explicit matrix formulas [28]. This is the exponential-family special case of the projection strat-egy: the bridge stays inside the finite-dimensional Gaussian manifold, whose dually flat structure makes every object explicit. The Light Schrödinger Bridge [97] extends this to arbitrary marginals by parametrising the Schrödinger potentials as log-sum-exp of quadratics — equivalently, Gaussian-mixture potentials — and optimising a single Kullback–Leibler objective in the energy-based-model formulation of entropic trans-port. The Gaussian-mixture parametrisation delivers a closed-form drift and lightspeed conditional sampling, dispensing with the simulation and MCMC inner loops of earlier diffusion-bridge solvers. In the language of this section, the potentials are confined to a mixture family — an 𝑚 -flat submanifold on which the 𝑒 -projection is computable in closed form. 

Multi-marginal bridges and variational inference on path space. The projection view extends to many marginals. Jiang [120] identifies continuous-time variational inference on path space — minimise 𝐷 KL (𝑄 ‖ 𝑃 ) over path measures 𝑄 matching ob-servations at several intermediate times — with a multi-marginal Schrödinger bridge, the optimal drift being a log 𝜑 -transform with 𝜑 solving a backward Kolmogorov equation, in direct extension of Theorem 4.1. The variational problem endows path space with an “Onsager–Fokker” metric in which the inverse diffusion tensor plays the role of the Fisher metric (4.54), tying the information geometry of this section to the Onsager–Machlup action of the fluctuation theory. Multi-marginal bridges are the natural setting for trajectory inference from snapshots, where data arrive at several time points and the intervening dynamics must be reconstructed. 132 4 Schrödinger Bridges and Information Geodesics in the Space of Probability Densities 

4.4.3 Dynamics on Statistical Manifolds: Accelerated Flows and Transport Hessians 

The bridge accelerates along the Fisher gradient. The second-order theme of Sect. 4.1.4 — that the Schrödinger bridge obeys a Newton-type law — has a sharp information-geometric form. Conforti [51] proved that, in the Riemannian structure of optimal transport, the entropic interpolation (𝜌 𝑡 ) solves a second-order equation whose right-hand side is the Wasserstein gradient of the Fisher information: loosely, 

•𝜌 𝑡 = 12 ∇𝑊 2 𝐼 (𝜌 𝑡 ), 𝐼 (𝜌 ) =

π

|∇ log 𝜌 |2 d𝜌, (4.62) “the acceleration of the bridge is the gradient of the Fisher information”. This is the same acceleration law as the Newton equation (4.24) of the local theory, now with the Fisher information — the information-geometric functional par excellence — as po-tential. From (4.62) Conforti derives a quantitative description of the bridge dynamics and a new functional inequality for the entropic transportation cost that generalises Ta-lagrand’s transport inequality, together with convexity of the Fisher information along the bridge whenever the associated reciprocal characteristic is convex. The first-order Otto picture and the entropic-cost functional inequalities of Sect. 4.1.5 are, from this vantage, two projections of one second-order object. 

Why the statistical manifold is easy. On the finite-dimensional statistical man-ifold the second-order theory is not merely available but classical. The curvatures of the 𝛼 -connections are explicit in derivatives of (4.56); the antisymmetric part 

Γ(− 𝛼 )

> 𝑖 𝑗,𝑘

= 𝛼 𝑇 𝑖 𝑗 𝑘 is governed entirely by the Amari–Chentsov tensor (4.55); and on a dually flat manifold the ∇(± 1) -curvatures vanish identically , so that 𝑒 - and 

𝑚 -geodesics are straight lines in their respective affine charts and parallel transport, Jacobi fields and projections reduce to linear algebra. The contrast with the open second-order problem of Sect. 4.3.4 has a one-word explanation, the last row of Ta-ble ?? : finite dimensionality . A parametric model is a finite-dimensional surface inside the infinite-dimensional density space, and its curvature theory is ordinary differential geometry — exactly as the graph simplex of Sect. 4.2.3 was. A programme of W. Li and collaborators, transport information geometry , systemat-ically fuses the transport and information geometries of this chapter at the second-order level. Its constructions pull information-geometric objects back through the Otto sub-mersion, turning statistical quantities into density-space dynamics. 

Accelerated information gradient flows. Nesterov’s accelerated gradient method discretises the damped second-order ODE •𝑥 + 𝛾 𝑡 §𝑥 + ∇ 𝑓 (𝑥 ) = 0. Wang and Li [214] lift this ODE to probability space for several information metrics — Fisher–Rao, Wasserstein-2, Kalman–Wasserstein and Stein — obtaining accelerated information gradient (AIG) flows. For the Wasserstein-2 metric the flow reads 4.4 Information Geometry 133 

𝜕 𝑡 𝜌 𝑡 + ∇· ( 𝜌 𝑡 𝑣 𝑡 ) = 0, 𝜕 𝑡 𝑣 𝑡 + 𝛾 𝑡 𝑣 𝑡 + 12 ∇| 𝑣 𝑡 |2 = −∇ 𝛿 F

𝛿𝜌 (𝜌 𝑡 ), (4.63) whose convective term 12 ∇| 𝑣 𝑡 |2 is the curvature correction of Wasserstein geome-try — the same term as in the Newton equation (4.24) and in (4.62). Wang and Li prove convergence properties of the accelerated flow for both the Fisher–Rao and the Wasserstein-2 metrics; the Fisher–Rao instance yields a momentum form of mean-field Langevin dynamics. The construction is the dynamical, density-space realisation of the acceleration that Nesterov’s method achieves in Euclidean space. 

Transport Hessians, Bregman divergences, and natural gradients. Beyond gra-dient flows, the transport Hessian of a functional, which is the second variation in Wasserstein geometry, defines new metrics and dynamics [147]: the transport-Hessian flow of the entropy is a Stein-type mean-field flow, and related transport-Hessian struc-tures reproduce classical equations of mathematical physics as second-order objects of density-space geometry. Companion constructions include transport Bregman diver-gences [149], whose entropy instance is a transport analogue of KL with closed Gaus-sian formulas, and the Wasserstein natural gradient [38, 154]: the pullback of the 𝑊 2

metric tensor to parameter space, which replaces Amari’s Fisher–Rao natural gradient by a transport-aware preconditioner for optimisation over parametric models. Finally, geodesic convexity of the KL divergence in the pulled-back geometry yields Ricci cur-vature lower bounds for parametric statistical models [155]. This is the Lott–Sturm– Villani theory of Sect. 4.1.5 transported onto finite-dimensional families, with explicit constants for exponential families. In every case the pattern of this section repeats: finite dimensionality converts second-order geometry into computable linear algebra. 

4.4.4 Information Geometry of Lévy Processes 

The nonlocal Wasserstein geometry discussed in the preceding sections treats a proba-bility density as a point in an infinite-dimensional transport space. A different geometric viewpoint arises when one fixes a parametric family of Lévy processes and regards the model parameters as coordinates on a finite-dimensional statistical manifold. The for-mer is equipped with a Fisher metric and a pair of dual affine connections derived from statistical divergences, whereas the latter is equipped with a nonlocal transport metric derived from a continuity equation and a kinetic action. Choi develops the information geometry of Lévy processes by first deriving the 

𝛼 -divergence between suitable equivalent process laws and then differentiating this divergence to obtain the Fisher information matrix and the associated 𝛼 -connections [44, 46]. This construction extends earlier calculations for tempered stable processes to a broader class of Lévy models. A parametric family of Lévy triplets (𝑏 𝜃 , 𝜎 2

Equations (4.64) and (4.65) should be interpreted as information-geometric formulas on the parameter manifold M. They are not nonlocal Wasserstein metric tensors on the space of probability densities. In particular, the tangent vectors in this subsection are parameter variations

rather than density variations represented by a local or nonlocal continuity equation. 

Tempered Stable and Financially Relevant Models. The general formulas are eval-uated in [44, 46] for several Lévy families used in financial modeling. The examples include generalized tempered stable processes, classical tempered stable processes, the CGMY model, and variance gamma processes. Choi also treats variance gamma pro-cesses through a limiting or regularized calculation adapted to the singular behavior of their Lévy measures. For these concrete models, the resulting metric and connection coefficients can be written in closed or semi-closed form in terms of the model pa-rameters and special functions. The cited works identify 𝑒 -flat structures for particular model families, including the classical tempered stable and variance gamma examples considered there. This is a model-dependent conclusion and should not be interpreted as a general dual-flatness theorem for all Lévy-process families. Likewise, the compu-tation of the Fisher metric and the 𝛼 -connections does not, by itself, provide a general closed-form solution of the corresponding geodesic equations. The information-geometric structure has direct statistical applications. The Fisher metric determines the Jeffreys prior 

𝜋 𝐽 (𝜃 ) ∝ √det 𝑔 (𝜃 ). (4.66) 4.4 Information Geometry 135 

For model families with an appropriate flat structure, the geometry can be used in pe-nalized likelihood methods for reducing the bias of maximum-likelihood estimators. In addition, the Laplace–Beltrami operator associated with the Fisher metric,

, (4.67) enters the construction of Bayesian predictive priors. In Komaki-type shrinkage con-structions, a positive superharmonic function with respect to Δ𝑔 is used to modify the Jeffreys prior and improve predictive performance under suitable conditions. The geometric results above provide a finite-dimensional differential geometry for distinguishing nearby Lévy models. They should be carefully separated from the contin-uous nonlocal Wasserstein geometry developed earlier in this chapter. Problem 4.1 (Gaussian Schrödinger bridges) Let 𝜇 0 = N ( 𝑚 0, Σ0) and 𝜇 1 =

N ( 𝑚 1, Σ1) on R𝑑 , with standard Brownian reference. Show that the entropic inter-polation remains Gaussian, 𝜌 𝑡 = N ( 𝑚 𝑡 , Σ𝑡 ), for all 𝑡 ∈ [ 0, 1]; identify the straight-line motion of the mean and the curved trajectory of the covariance, and compare with the displacement interpolation as 𝜀 → 0. Hint: linear reference dynamics preserve quadratic forms in the forward–backward system (4.16)–(4.17); see [28]. 

Problem 4.2 ( 𝛼 -divergences unify the KL pair) Prove that the 𝛼 -divergence (4.60) converges to the forward KL divergence as 𝛼 → 1 and to the reverse KL divergence as 

𝛼 → − 1, and interpret the two limits (mode-covering versus mode-seeking) for a model fitted by minimising 𝐷 ( 𝛼 ) . Hint: l’Hôpital in 𝛼 .

Problem 4.4 (Bending the metric) Explain, at the level of the metric tensor, how the “speed limit” for transporting mass between two separated clusters changes when the 136 4 Schrödinger Bridges and Information Geodesics in the Space of Probability Densities

Problem 4.5 (Early warning on the density manifold) Classical early-warning signals for critical transitions track critical slowing down (rising variance and autocorrelation) and are unreliable under non-Gaussian forcing. Discuss how casting the transition as a finite-time Schrödinger bridge between the healthy and the tipped distributions provides an alternative indicator — the decay of the required steering energy / bridge distance — that remains meaningful for Lévy perturbations, in the light of Example 4.2. See [223, 229].
